# New Jersey

This article is about the U.S. state. For other uses, see [New Jersey (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_\(disambiguation\)).

"NJ" redirects here. For other uses, see [NJ (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NJ_\(disambiguation\)).

Ubiquitous [gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden) exemplified by [landscaping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landscaping) at [Rutgers University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutgers_University-Camden)-[Camden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camden,_New_Jersey) lend New Jersey its nickname of the Garden State.

**New Jersey** is a [state](18618239.md) located in both the [Mid-Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-Atlantic_States) and [Northeastern](431669.md) regions of the United States. Located at the geographic hub of the [heavily urbanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_area) [Northeast megalopolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_megalopolis), it is bordered to the northwest, north, and northeast by [New York State](8210131.md); on its east, southeast, and south by the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md); on its west by the [Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River) and [Pennsylvania](23332.md); and on its southwest by [Delaware Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_Bay) and [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware). At 7,354 square miles (19,050 km<sup>2</sup>), New Jersey is the [fifth-smallest U.S. state by land area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_area). According to a 2025 [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) estimate, it is the [11th-most populous state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_population), with over 9.5 million residents, its highest estimated count ever. The state capital is [Trenton](57843.md), and the state's most populous city is [Newark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newark,_New_Jersey). New Jersey is the [most densely populated U.S. state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_states_and_territories_of_the_United_States_by_population_density), and is the only U.S. state in which every [county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_New_Jersey) is deemed [urban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_area) by the U.S. Census Bureau.

New Jersey was first inhabited by [Paleo-Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleo-Indians) as early as 13,000 BC. The [Lenape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenape) were the dominant [Indigenous group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) when Europeans arrived in the early 17th century, and they were subdivided into dialectal groups such as the Munsee, in the north, and the Unami and the Unalachtigo, elsewhere.[^cite_note-9][^cite_note-10] [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Republic) and [Swedish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Empire) colonists founded the first European settlements in the state,[^cite_note-11] with the [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England) later seizing control of the region and establishing the [Province of New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_New_Jersey), named after the [Channel Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_Islands) of [Jersey](15693.md).[^cite_note-12][^cite_note-13] The colony's fertile lands and relative [religious tolerance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toleration) drew a large and diverse population. New Jersey was among the [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies) that supported the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution), hosting several [pivotal battles and military commands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_in_the_American_Revolution) in the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War) earning the title "Crossroads of the American Revolution".[^cite_note-Crossroads-14] New Jersey remained in the [Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_\(American_Civil_War\)) during the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) and [provided](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_in_the_American_Civil_War) troops, resources, and military leaders in support of the [Union Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Army). After the war, the state emerged as a major [manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manufacturing) center and a leading destination for immigrants, helping drive the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) in the U.S. New Jersey was the site of many industrial, technological, and commercial [innovations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innovation).[^cite_note-15] [Many prominent Americans associated with New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_from_New_Jersey) have proven influential nationally and globally, including in [academia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_institutions), social advocacy, business, entertainment, government, military, [non-profit leadership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-profit_organization), and other fields.

New Jersey's central location in the Northeast megalopolis helped fuel its rapid growth and [suburbanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburbanization) in the second half of the 20th century. Since the beginning of the 21st century, the state's economy has become highly diversified, with major sectors, including New Jersey's role as the world's largest [biopharmaceutical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biopharmaceutical) industry hub, and is widely known as the "Medicine Chest of the World".[^cite_note-phar-16] New Jersey excels in [biotechnology](4502.md), [information technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_technology), [STEM education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STEM), [finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street_West), [digital media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_media), [filmmaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filmmaking), and [tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism), and it has become an [Atlantic seaboard](89126.md) epicenter for [logistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logistics) and [distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distribution_\(marketing\)). New Jersey is a major destination for immigrants and is home to one of the world's most [multicultural populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_enclave). Since the onset of the 21st century, re-urbanization is taking place in New Jersey, particularly near [New Jersey Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_Transit) [rail hubs](6286.md).[^cite_note-Re22-17][^cite_note-Re3-18][^cite_note-19] New Jersey is the fastest-growing state in the [U.S. Northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Northeast) and one of the fastest-growing in the United States overall, attributed to its resilient economy and aggressive [homebuilding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homebuilding) policies.[^cite_note-Ngrow-20]

New Jersey is one of the most developed states in the U.S. New Jersey had a [median household income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_income) of \$99,781 as of 2023, the second-highest of any U.S. state behind [Massachusetts](1645518.md).[^cite_note-NJWealth-21] Almost one-tenth of all households in the state, or over 323,000, are [millionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millionaire), the highest representation of millionaires among all states and the highest density per square mile.[^cite_note-Millionaires2-22] Despite not being a U.S. border state, one-quarter of New Jersey's population is [foreign born](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_born), the second-highest proportion after [California](5407.md).[^cite_note-Foreign-23] The state ranks near the top on both the [American Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_American_Human_Development_Index) and the [standard Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_Human_Development_Index). New Jersey has been ranked the top U.S. state in physical and [emotional wellness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_health), despite being one of the most expensive states for residents to live in.[^cite_note-24]

According to [climatology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climatology) research by the U.S. [National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration](37876.md), New Jersey has been the [fastest-warming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming) state by average [air temperature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_temperature) over a 100-year period beginning in the early 20th century, which has been attributed to significant warming of the [North Atlantic Ocean](698.md),[^cite_note-warm-25] and the coastal waters of the [Jersey Shore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_Shore) constitute one of the fastest-warming urban ocean [shorelines](5236.md) in the world.[^cite_note-Nshore-26]

## History

Main article: [History of New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_New_Jersey)

### Prehistoric era

See also: [Paleontology in New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleontology_in_New_Jersey)

The pressure of collision between [North America](21139.md) and [Africa](5334607.md) gave rise to the [Appalachian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian_Mountains). Around 18,000 years ago, the [Ice Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_glaciation) resulted in [glaciers](12463.md) that reached New Jersey. As glaciers retreated, they left behind [Lake Passaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Passaic) along with rivers, [meadows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meadow), swamps, and [gorges](78478.md).[^cite_note-27]

Since the [6th millennium BC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6th_millennium_BC), [Native American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) people have inhabited New Jersey, beginning with the [Lenape tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenape). *_Scheyichbi_* is the [Lenape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unami_language) name for the land that represents present-day New Jersey.[^cite_note-28] The Lenape were several [autonomous](191145.md) groups that practiced [maize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize) agriculture in order to supplement their hunting and gathering in the region surrounding the [Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River), the lower [Hudson River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_River), and western [Long Island Sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Island_Sound). The Lenape were divided into [matrilineal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matrilineality) clans that were based upon common female ancestors. Clans were organized into three distinct [phratries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phratry) identified by their animal sign: [Turtle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turtle), [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey_\(bird\)), and [Wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_wolf). They first encountered the [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_colonization_of_the_Americas) in the early 17th century, and their primary relationship with the Dutch and later European settlers was through [fur trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fur_trade).

### Colonial era

Main articles: [Colonial history of New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_history_of_New_Jersey), [New Netherland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Netherland), [New Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Sweden), [Province of New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_New_Jersey), [East Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Jersey), [West Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Jersey), and [Dominion of New England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_New_England)



A map of [New Netherland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Netherland) and [New Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Sweden) in present-day New Jersey during the [colonial era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_history_of_New_Jersey)

The [Dutch](21148.md) were the first [Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Europe) to lay claim to geographic territory in present-day New Jersey. The Dutch colony of [New Netherland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Netherland) consisted of parts of the modern [Mid-Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-Atlantic_\(United_States\)) states. Although the European principle of [land ownership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_ownership) was not recognized by the [Lenape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenape), [Dutch West India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_West_India_Company) policy required its colonists to purchase land that they settled. The first to do so was [Michiel Pauw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michiel_Reyniersz_Pauw), who established a [patroonship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patroon) called [Pavonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavonia,_New_Netherland) in 1630 along [North River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_River_\(Hudson_River\)), that eventually became [Bergen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bergen,_New_Netherland), the first permanent settlement, local civil government, and municipality in the state. [Peter Minuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Minuit)'s purchase of lands along the [Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River) established the colony of [New Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Sweden), that lasted until the Dutch conquered it in 1655. The entire region became a territory of [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England) on June 24, 1664, after an English fleet under command of Colonel [Richard Nicolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nicolls) sailed into what is now [New York Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Harbor) and took control of [Fort Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Amsterdam), annexing the entire province.

During the [English Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Civil_War), the [Channel Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_Islands) of [Jersey](15693.md) remained loyal to the [British Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crown) and gave sanctuary to the [King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_England). In the Royal Square in [St Helier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Helier), [Charles II of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_II_of_England) was proclaimed King in 1649, following the execution of his father, [Charles I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_I_of_England). North American lands were divided by [Charles II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_II_of_England), who gave his brother, the Duke of York (later [King James II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_II_of_England)), the region between [New England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England) and [Maryland](18858.md) as a [proprietary colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proprietary_colony) (as opposed to a [royal colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_colony)). James then granted land between the [Hudson River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_River) and the [Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River) (the land that would become New Jersey) to two friends who had remained loyal through the [English Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Civil_War): [Sir George Carteret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Carteret) and [Lord Berkeley of Stratton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Berkeley,_1st_Baron_Berkeley_of_Stratton).[^cite_note-29] The area was named the [Province of New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_New_Jersey).

Since its inception, New Jersey has been characterized by [ethnic and religious diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism). New England [Congregationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregationalists) settled alongside [Scots Presbyterians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots'_Church,_Melbourne) and [Dutch Reformed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Reformed) migrants. While the majority of residents lived in towns with individual [landholdings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landowner) of 100 acres (40 ha), a few rich proprietors owned vast estates. English [Quakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quakers) and [Anglicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicans) owned large landholdings. Unlike [Plymouth Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plymouth_Colony), [Jamestown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamestown,_Virginia) and other colonies, New Jersey was populated by a secondary wave of immigrants who came from other colonies instead of those who migrated directly from Europe. New Jersey remained agrarian and rural throughout the colonial era, and [commercial farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_agriculture) developed sporadically. Some townships, such as [Burlington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burlington,_New_Jersey) on the Delaware River and [Perth Amboy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perth_Amboy), emerged as important ports for shipping to New York City and Philadelphia. The colony's fertile lands and [tolerant religious policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toleration) drew more settlers, and the New Jersey population had increased to 120,000 by 1775.

Settlement for the first ten years of English rule took place along the [Hackensack River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hackensack_River) and [Arthur Kill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Kill). Settlers came primarily from New York and New England. On March 18, 1673, Berkeley sold his half of the colony to [Quakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_Society_of_Friends) in England, who settled the [Delaware Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_Valley) region as a Quaker colony, with [William Penn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Penn) acting as [trustee](506701.md) for the lands for a time. New Jersey was governed as two distinct provinces, [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Jersey) and [West Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Jersey), for 28 years between 1674 and 1702, which were part of the [Dominion of New England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_New_England) from 1686 to 1689.

In 1702, the two provinces were reunited under a [royal governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Governor_of_New_Jersey) rather than a [proprietary one](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proprietary_governor). [Edward Hyde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Hyde,_3rd_Earl_of_Clarendon), titled Lord Cornbury, became the first governor of the royal colony. Britain believed that he was an ineffective and corrupt ruler, taking bribes and speculating on land. In 1708, he was recalled to England. New Jersey was then ruled by the governors of New York, but this infuriated the settlers of New Jersey, who accused these governors of favoritism to New York. [Judge Lewis Morris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_Morris_\(governor\)) led the case for a separate governor, and was appointed governor by [King George II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_II_of_Great_Britain) in 1738.[^cite_note-30]

### Revolutionary War era

Main articles: [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War), [Northern theater of the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_theater_of_the_American_Revolutionary_War), [New Jersey in the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_in_the_American_Revolution), [Lee Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Resolution), [United States Declaration of Independence](31874.md), [Philadelphia campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_campaign), [Articles of Confederation § Ratification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Articles_of_Confederation#Ratification), [Treaty of Paris (1783)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1783\)), [Constitutional Convention (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Convention_\(United_States\)), [Admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admission_to_the_Union), and [List of U.S. states by date of admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_date_of_admission_to_the_Union)



*[Washington Crossing the Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Crossing_the_Delaware_\(1851_painting\))*, an 1851 portrait by [Emanuel Leutze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emanuel_Leutze) depicting [Washington's covert crossing the Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington's_crossing_of_the_Delaware_River) from [Bucks County, Pennsylvania](91976.md) to [Mercer County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercer_County,_New_Jersey) on December 25, 1776, prior to the [Battle of Trenton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Trenton)



*Washington Rallying the Americans at the Battle of Princeton*, a portrait by [William Ranney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Ranney) depicting [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) rallying [Continental Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Army) troops at the [Battle of Princeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Princeton) in January 1777

New Jersey was one of the [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies) that revolted against British rule in the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution) and declared independence from British rule. The [New Jersey Constitution of 1776](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/New%20Jersey%20Constitution%20of%201776) was passed July 2, 1776, just two days before the [Second Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Continental_Congress) declared American Independence from [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain). It was an act of the [Provincial Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provincial_Congress_of_New_Jersey), which made itself into the [State Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_Legislature). To reassure neutrals, the new constitution provided that the legislature would disband if New Jersey reached reconciliation with Great Britain. Among the 56 [founders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers) who signed the [Declaration of Independence](31874.md), five were New Jersey representatives: [Richard Stockton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Stockton_\(Continental_Congressman\)), [John Witherspoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Witherspoon), [Francis Hopkinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Hopkinson), [John Hart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hart_\(New_Jersey_politician\)), and [Abraham Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Clark).

During the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War), British and American armies crossed New Jersey numerous times with several pivotal battles taking place in the state. In total, there were 296 engagements that occurred within New Jersey, more clashes than occurred in any other of the 13 colonies during the war.[^cite_note-Crossroads-14] Because of this, New Jersey today is sometimes referred to as "The Crossroads of the American Revolution".[^cite_note-31] The winter quarters of the [Continental Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Army) were established in New Jersey twice by General [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) in [Morristown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morristown,_New_Jersey), which has been called "The Military Capital of the American Revolution".[^cite_note-32]

On the night of December 25–26, 1776, the Continental Army under [George Washington crossed the Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington's_crossing_of_the_Delaware_River). After the crossing, they surprised and defeated the [Hessian troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hessian_\(soldiers\)) in the [Battle of Trenton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Trenton). Slightly more than a week after victory at [Trenton](57843.md), Continental Army forces gained an important victory by stopping [General Cornwallis's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Cornwallis,_1st_Marquess_Cornwallis) charges at the [Second Battle of Trenton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Battle_of_Trenton). Evading Cornwallis's army, the Continental Army launched a surprise attack on [Princeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton,_New_Jersey) and defeated the British on January 3, 1777. [Emanuel Leutze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emanuel_Leutze)'s painting of *[Washington Crossing the Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Crossing_the_Delaware_\(1851_painting\))* became an icon of the Revolution.

Continental Army forces under Washington's command met British forces under General [Henry Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Clinton_\(American_War_of_Independence\)) at the [Battle of Monmouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Monmouth) in an indecisive engagement in June 1778. Washington's forces attempted to take the British column by surprise. When the British army attempted to flank the Americans, the Continental Army retreated in disorder. Their ranks were later reorganized and withstood British charges.[^cite_note-33]

In the summer of 1783, the [Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Congress) met in [Nassau Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nassau_Hall) at [Princeton University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_University), making [Princeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton,_New_Jersey) the nation's capital for four months. It was there that the Continental Congress learned of the signing of the [Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1783\)), which ended the war.

On December 18, 1787, New Jersey became the third state to ratify the [United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Constitution), which was overwhelmingly popular in New Jersey since it prevented New York and Pennsylvania from charging [tariffs](55551.md) on goods imported from Europe. On November 20, 1789, New Jersey became the first in the newly formed Union to ratify the [Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights).[^cite_note-34]

The [New Jersey 1776 State Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_New_Jersey) gave the vote to all inhabitants who had a certain level of wealth or owned property. Both parties passed the Voting Rights Acts. This included women and Black people, excluding married women who were not legally permitted to own property separately from their husbands. Unmarried women retained their suffrage in the new state of [New Jersey](21648.md) from 1776 to 1807.[^cite_note-35] Nonetheless, in several elections both sides claimed that their opposing candidates were without merit and were trying to win just by influencing women voters and they accused their opponents of seeking "petticoat electors". In 1807, the state legislature passed a bill interpreting the constitution to mean universal white male [suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffrage), excluding paupers; the constitution was itself an act of the legislature and not enshrined as the modern constitution.[^cite_note-36]

### 19th century

Main articles: [New Jersey in the 19th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_in_the_19th_century) and [New Jersey in the American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_in_the_American_Civil_War)



Map of the 107-mile-long (172 km) [Morris Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morris_Canal), which crosses the state

On February 15, 1804, New Jersey became the last northern state to [abolish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States) new [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery) and enacted legislation that slowly phased out existing slavery. This led to a gradual decrease of the slave population. By the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War)'s end, about a dozen African Americans in New Jersey were still held in bondage.[^cite_note-37] New Jersey voters eventually ratified the constitutional amendments banning slavery and granting rights to the United States' black population.

Industrialization accelerated in the present-day [North Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Jersey) region of the state following completion of the [Morris Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morris_Canal) in 1831. The canal allowed for [anthracite coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthracite) to be transported from eastern [Pennsylvania](23332.md)'s [Lehigh Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lehigh_Valley) to North Jersey's growing industries in [Paterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paterson,_New_Jersey), [Newark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newark,_New_Jersey), and [Jersey City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_City,_New_Jersey).

In 1844, the second [state constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_State_Constitution) was ratified and brought into effect. Counties thereby became districts for the state senate, and some realignment of boundaries (including the creation of [Mercer County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercer_County,_New_Jersey)) immediately followed. This provision was retained in the 1947 Constitution, but was overturned by the [Supreme Court of the United States](31737.md) in 1962, by the decision *[Baker v. Carr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baker_v._Carr)*. While the Governorship was stronger than under the 1776 constitution, the 1844 constitution created many offices that were not responsible to the governor or the people. Furthermore, it limited him to a single three-year term.

New Jersey was one of the few Union states (the others being [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware) and [Kentucky](16846.md)) to select a candidate other than [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln) twice in national elections, and sided with [Stephen A. Douglas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_A._Douglas) (1860) and [George B. McClellan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_B._McClellan) (1864) during their campaigns. McClellan, a native [Philadelphian](50585.md), had New Jersey ties and formally resided in New Jersey at the time; he later became Governor of New Jersey (1878–81). During the American Civil War, the state was led by Republican governor [Charles Smith Olden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Smith_Olden) and later by Democrat [Joel Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joel_Parker_\(politician\)). Between 65,000 and 80,000 state soldiers enlisted in the Union army; unlike many other states, it saw no battles.[^cite_note-38]

In the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution), cities like Paterson grew and prospered. Previously, the economy had been largely agrarian, which was problematically subject to crop failures and poor soil. This caused a shift to a more [industrialized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialization) economy, one based on manufactured commodities such as textiles and silk. [Inventor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_inventors) [Thomas Edison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Edison) also became an important figure of the Industrial Revolution, having been [granted 1,093 patents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Edison_patents), many of which for inventions he developed while working in New Jersey. Edison's facilities, first at [Menlo Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menlo_Park,_New_Jersey) and then in [West Orange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Orange,_New_Jersey), are considered perhaps the first [research centers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_center) in the United States. Christie Street in Menlo Park was the first thoroughfare in the world to have electric lighting. Transportation was greatly improved as [locomotion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Locomotive) and [steamboats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steamboat) were introduced to New Jersey.

[Iron](145352.md) mining was also a leading industry during the middle to late 19th century. [Bog iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bog_iron) pits in the [New Jersey Pine Barrens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_Pine_Barrens) were among the first sources of iron for the new nation.[^cite_note-39] Mines such as Mt. Hope, Mine Hill, and the Rockaway Valley Mines created a thriving industry. Mining generated the impetus for new towns and was one of the driving forces behind the need for the [Morris Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morris_Canal). [Zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc) mines were also a major industry, especially the [Sterling Hill Mine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sterling_Hill_Mine).

### 20th century

Main article: [New Jersey in the 20th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_in_the_20th_century)

New Jersey prospered through the [Roaring Twenties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roaring_Twenties). The first [Miss America Pageant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miss_America_Pageant) was held in 1921 in [Atlantic City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_City); the [Holland Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holland_Tunnel) connecting [Jersey City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_City,_New_Jersey) to [Manhattan](45470.md) opened in 1927; and the first [drive-in movie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drive-in_movie) was shown in 1933 in [Camden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camden,_New_Jersey). During the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) of the 1930s, the state offered begging licenses to unemployed residents,[^cite_note-40] the zeppelin airship [Hindenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindenburg_disaster) crashed in flames over [Lakehurst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakehurst,_New_Jersey), and the [SS *Morro Castle*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SS_Morro_Castle_\(1930\)) beached itself near [Asbury Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asbury_Park,_New_Jersey) after going up in flames while at sea.

Through both [World Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War), New Jersey was a center for war production, especially naval construction. The [Federal Shipbuilding and Drydock Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Shipbuilding_and_Drydock_Company) yards in Kearny and Newark and the [New York Shipbuilding Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Shipbuilding_Corporation) yard in Camden produced [aircraft carriers](2219.md), battleships, cruisers, and destroyers.[^cite_note-41] New Jersey manufactured 6.8 percent of total United States military armaments produced during [World War II](32927.md), ranking fifth among the 48 states.[^cite_note-42] In addition, [Fort Dix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Dix,_New_Jersey) (1917) (originally called "Camp Dix"),[^cite_note-dixhist-43] [Camp Merritt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Merritt_\(New_Jersey\)) (1917),[^cite_note-44] and [Camp Kilmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Kilmer) (1941) were all constructed to house and train American soldiers through both World Wars. New Jersey also became a principal location for defense in the [Cold War](325329.md). Fourteen [Nike missile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Nike) stations were constructed for the defense of the New York City and [Philadelphia](50585.md) areas. *[PT-109](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motor_Torpedo_Boat_PT-109)*, a motor torpedo boat commanded by Lt. (j.g.) [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy) in World War II, was built at the Elco Boatworks in [Bayonne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayonne,_New_Jersey). The aircraft carrier [USS *Enterprise* (CV-6)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Enterprise_\(CV-6\)) was briefly docked at the Military Ocean Terminal in Bayonne in the 1950s before she was sent to Kearney to be scrapped.[^cite_note-45] In 1962, the world's first nuclear-powered cargo ship, the [NS Savannah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NS_Savannah), was launched at Camden.

In 1951, the [New Jersey Turnpike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_Turnpike) opened, facilitating efficient travel by car and truck between [North Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Jersey) and the [New York metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_metropolitan_area), and [South Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Jersey) and the [Philadelphia metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_metropolitan_area).[^cite_note-46] Subsequently, in 1957, the [Garden State Parkway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_State_Parkway) was completed, serving as a diagonal counterpart to the Turnpike, and opening up highway travel along New Jersey's coastal flank between [Bergen County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bergen_County,_New_Jersey) in the northeast and the [Cape May County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_May_County,_New_Jersey) [peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_May_peninsula) at the southeastern tip of New Jersey; in doing so, the [Jersey Shore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_Shore) became readily accessible to millions of residents in the New York metropolitan area. In 1959, Air Defense Command deployed the [CIM-10 Bomarc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIM-10_Bomarc) [surface-to-air missile](182664.md) to [McGuire Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McGuire_Air_Force_Base). On June 7, 1960, an [explosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BOMARC_Missile_Accident_Site) in a CIM-10 Bomarc missile fuel tank caused an accident and subsequent plutonium contamination.[^cite_note-47]

In the 1960s, [race riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_riot) erupted in many of the industrial cities of North Jersey. The first race riots in New Jersey occurred in Jersey City on August 2, 1964. Several others ensued in 1967, in [Newark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Newark_riots) and [Plainfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Plainfield_riots). [Other riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_assassination_riots) followed the [assassination of Martin Luther King Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Martin_Luther_King_Jr.) in April 1968, just as in the rest of the country. A [riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camden_Riot_of_1971) occurred in [Camden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camden,_New_Jersey) in 1971. As a result of an order from the [New Jersey Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_Supreme_Court) to fund schools equitably, the New Jersey legislature passed an income tax bill in 1976. Prior to this bill, the state had no income tax.[^cite_note-tws29decf3354-48]

### 21st century

Main article: [New Jersey in the 21st century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_in_the_21st_century)

&#x20;In the early part of the 2000s, two [light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail) systems were opened: the [Hudson–Bergen Light Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson%E2%80%93Bergen_Light_Rail) (HBLR) in [Hudson County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_County) and the [River Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Line_\(NJ_Transit\)) between [Camden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camden,_New_Jersey) and [Trenton](57843.md). The intent of these projects was to encourage [transit-oriented development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transit-oriented_development) in [North Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Jersey) and [South Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Jersey), respectively. The HBLR was credited with a revitalization of [Hudson County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_County,_New_Jersey) and [Jersey City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_City,_New_Jersey).[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] Urban revitalization has continued in North Jersey in the 21st century. In 2014, Jersey City's Census-estimated population was 262,146,[^cite_note-PopEst-53] with the largest population increase of any municipality in New Jersey since 2010,[^cite_note-JC2014Est-54] representing an increase of 5.9% from the [2010 U.S. census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census), when the city's population was enumerated at 247,597.[^cite_note-Census2010-55][^cite_note-LWD2010-56] In 2017, Jersey City's tax base grew by US\$136 million, giving Jersey City the largest municipal tax base in New Jersey.[^cite_note-57] Between [2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_census) and [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census) Newark experienced its first population increase since the 1950s, and by [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census) had rebounded to 311,549.

## Geography

[Map](21648.md)

Interactive map of New Jersey

Main articles: [Geography of New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_New_Jersey) and [Climate of New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_New_Jersey)

See also: [List of counties in New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_New_Jersey) and [Metropolitan Statistical Areas of New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Statistical_Areas_of_New_Jersey)





Sunrise on the [Jersey Shore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_Shore) at [Spring Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_Lake,_New_Jersey) in [Monmouth County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monmouth_County,_New_Jersey) (top) and sunset at [Sunset Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunset_Beach,_New_Jersey) in [Cape May County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_May_County,_New_Jersey) (bottom)

New Jersey is at the center of the [Northeast megalopolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_megalopolis), the most populated American [urban agglomeration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_agglomeration). It is bordered to the north and northeast by [New York](8210131.md) (parts of which are across the [Hudson River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_River), [Upper New York Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_New_York_Bay), the [Kill Van Kull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kill_Van_Kull), [Newark Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newark_Bay), and the [Arthur Kill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Kill)); to the east by the Atlantic Ocean; to the southwest by [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware) across [Delaware Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_Bay); and to the west by [Pennsylvania](23332.md) across the [Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River).

New Jersey is broadly divided into the [North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Jersey), [Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Jersey), and [South Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Jersey) geographic regions, although some residents do not consider Central Jersey a region in its own right. Across the regions are five distinct areas divided by natural geography and population concentration. Northeastern New Jersey, often referred to as the [Gateway Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gateway_Region), lies closest to [Manhattan](45470.md) in New York City, and up to a million residents commute daily into the city for work, many via public transportation.[^cite_note-69] The [Jersey Shore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_Shore), along the Atlantic Coast in Central and South Jersey, has its own unique natural, residential, and cultural characteristics owing to its location by the ocean. South Jersey represents the southernmost geographical region of the [northeastern United States](431669.md). The [Philadelphia metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_metropolitan_area) includes the southwestern counties of the state, which reside within the Delaware Valley surrounding [Philadelphia](50585.md).

Despite the state's heavily urban character and a long history of [industrialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialization), forests cover roughly 45 percent of New Jersey's land area, or approximately 2.1 million acres (8,500 km<sup>2</sup>), [ranking 31st](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_cover_by_state_and_territory_in_the_United_States) among the 50 U.S. states and six territories.[^cite_note-70] Northwestern New Jersey, often referred to as the [Skylands Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skylands_Region), is more wooded, rural, and mountainous. The chief tree of the northern forests is the [oak](39696.md). The [New Jersey Pine Barrens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_Pine_Barrens) is situated in the southern interior of New Jersey and covered extensively by mixed [pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pine) and [oak](39696.md) forest; its population density is lower than most of the state.

[High Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Point_\(New_Jersey\)) in [Montague Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montague_Township,_New_Jersey), [Sussex County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sussex_County,_New_Jersey) is the state's highest elevation at 1,803 feet (550 m) above sea level. The state's highest [prominence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prominence_\(mountains\)) is [Kitty Ann Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kitty_Ann_Mountain) in [Morris County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morris_County,_New_Jersey), rising 892 feet (272 m). The [Palisades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Palisades_\(Hudson_River\)) are a line of steep cliffs on the west side of the [Hudson River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_River) in [Bergen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bergen_County,_New_Jersey) and [Hudson Counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_County,_New_Jersey). Major [New Jersey rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_New_Jersey_rivers) include the [Hudson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_River), [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River), [Raritan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raritan_River), [Passaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passaic_River), [Hackensack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hackensack_River), [Rahway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahway_River), [Musconetcong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musconetcong_River), [Mullica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mullica_River), [Rancocas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rancocas_River), [Manasquan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manasquan_River), [Maurice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_River), and [Toms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toms_River) rivers. Due to New Jersey's [peninsular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsula) geography, both sunrise and sunset are visible over water from different points on the Jersey Shore.

Despite being the [most densely populated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_density) [U.S. state](18618239.md), New Jersey is mostly suburban.

* The Jersey Shore extends inland from the Atlantic Ocean into its many inlets, including Manasquan Inlet, looking westward at sunset from the jetty at Manasquan.

  The [Jersey Shore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_Shore) extends inland from the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md) into its many [inlets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inlet), including [Manasquan Inlet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manasquan_Inlet), looking westward at [sunset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunset) from the [jetty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jetty) at [Manasquan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manasquan,_New_Jersey).

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_New_Jersey)

The state consists of two climate zones; most of the state has a [humid subtropical climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical_climate) (Cfa), while the northwest has a [humid continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate) (Dfa).[^cite_note-Koppen-71] New Jersey receives between 2,400 and 2,800 hours of [sunshine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunshine) annually.[^cite_note-72]

Summers are typically hot and humid, with statewide average high temperatures of 82–87 °F (28–31 °C) and lows of 60–69 °F (16–21 °C); however, temperatures exceed 90 °F (32 °C) on average 25 days each summer, exceeding 100 °F (38 °C) in some years. Winters are usually cold, with average high temperatures of 34–43 °F (1–6 °C) and lows of 16 to 28 °F (−9 to −2 °C) for most of the state, but temperatures can, for brief periods, fall below 10 °F (−12 °C) and sometimes rise above 50 °F (10 °C). Northwestern parts of the state have significantly colder winters with sub-0 °F (−18 °C) being an almost annual occurrence. Spring and autumn may feature wide temperature variations, with lower humidity than summer. The [USDA Plant Hardiness Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardiness_zone) classification ranges from 6 in the northwest of the state, to 7B near Cape May.[^cite_note-73] All-time temperature extremes recorded in New Jersey include 110 °F (43 °C) on July 10, 1936, in [Runyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Runyon,_New_Jersey), Middlesex County and −34 °F (−37 °C) on January 5, 1904, in [River Vale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Vale,_New_Jersey), Bergen County.[^cite_note-74]

Average annual precipitation ranges from 43 to 51 inches (1,100 to 1,300 mm), spread uniformly throughout the year. Average snowfall per winter season ranges from 10–15 inches (25–38 cm) in the south and near the seacoast, 15–30 inches (38–76 cm) in the northeast and central part of the state, to about 40–50 inches (1.0–1.3 m) in the northwestern highlands, but this often varies considerably from year to year. Precipitation falls on an average of 120 days a year, with 25 to 30 thunderstorms, most of which occur during the summer.

During winter and early spring, New Jersey can experience [nor'easters](402179.md), which are capable of causing [blizzards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blizzards) or flooding throughout the northeastern United States. [Hurricanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane) and [tropical storms](8282374.md), [tornadoes](37530.md), and earthquakes are rare; the state was impacted by a hurricane in [1903](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1903_New_Jersey_hurricane), Tropical Storm [Floyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Floyd) in [1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Atlantic_hurricane_season),[^cite_note-75] and [Hurricane Sandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Sandy) in [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Atlantic_hurricane_season), which made landfall in the state with top winds of 90 mph (145 km/h).

#### Climate change

[Climate change is affecting New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_New_Jersey) faster than much of the rest of the United States. [Climatologists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climatologist) at the U.S. [National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration](37876.md) have concluded that New Jersey has been the fastest-warming state by average air temperature over a 100-year period beginning in the early 20th century.[^cite_note-warm-25]

### Administrative divisions

See also: [List of counties in New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_New_Jersey) and [List of municipalities in New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_New_Jersey)

The [United States Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) divides New Jersey's 21 counties into seven [metropolitan statistical areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Statistical_Areas_of_New_Jersey), with 20 counties included in either the New York City or Philadelphia [Combined Statistical Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_Statistical_Area). [Warren County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_County,_New_Jersey) is part of the [Lehigh Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lehigh_Valley) metropolitan area based in eastern [Pennsylvania](23332.md).

#### Municipalities

For its overall population and nation-leading population density, New Jersey is primarily [suburban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburban) and has a relative paucity of classically large cities. This [paradox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paradox) is most pronounced in [Bergen County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bergen_County,_New_Jersey), the state's most populous county, whose 955,732 residents at the 2020 census inhabited 70 municipalities, of which the most populous is [Hackensack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hackensack,_New_Jersey), with 46,030 residents. Many urban areas extend far beyond the limits of a single large city, as New Jersey municipalities tend to be geographically small; three of the four largest cities in New Jersey by population have under 20 square miles (52 km<sup>2</sup>) of land area, and eight of the top ten, including all the top five, have a land area under 30 square miles (78 km<sup>2</sup>). As of the [2010 United States census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census), only four municipalities had over 100,000 residents (although Edison and Woodbridge Township came very close); this number increased to seven by the 2020 census.

## Demographics

### Population

Residents of New Jersey are most commonly referred to as New Jerseyans or, less commonly, as New Jerseyites. According to the [2020 U.S. census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census), the state had a population of 9,288,994, a 5.7% increase since the [2010 U.S. census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census), which counted 8,791,894 residents.[^cite_note-QuickFactsNJ-4] The state ranked eleventh in the country by total population and first in population density, with 1,185 residents per square mile (458 per km<sup>2</sup>). Historically, New Jersey has experienced one of the fastest growth rates in the country, with its population increasing by double digits almost every decade until 1980; growth has since slowed but remained relatively robust until recently. In 2022, the Census Bureau estimated there were 6,262 fewer residents than in 2020, a decline of 0.3% from 2020, related to the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md).[^cite_note-quickfacts-79] In 2024, the Census Bureau reported that the state population rebounded by 2.3% or 211,837 residents from April 2020. New Jersey lead the [Northeast](431669.md) with a 1.3% growth rate or 121,209 residents from 2023 placing it among the top ten states nationwide for population growth.[^cite_note-Growth-80]

New Jersey is the only state where every county is deemed [urban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_area) as defined by the [Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau).[^cite_note-81] Most residents live in the counties surrounding [New York City](645042.md), the nation's largest city, [Philadelphia](50585.md), the nation's sixth-largest city, or along the eastern [Jersey Shore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_Shore); the extreme southern and northwestern counties are relatively less dense overall. Since the 2000 census, the [United States Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) calculated that New Jersey's [center of population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_of_population) was located in [East Brunswick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Brunswick,_New_Jersey).[^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84] The state is located in the middle of the [Northeast megalopolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_megalopolis), which has more than 50 million residents.

As of 2023, New Jersey had a [median household income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_income) of \$99,781, the [second-highest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_income) of any U.S. state behind [Massachusetts](1645518.md).[^cite_note-NJMedianIncome-85][^cite_note-QF_table-86] Conversely, New Jersey's poverty rate of 9.4% was slightly lower than the national average of 11.4%,[^cite_note-QF_table-86] and the [sixth lowest of the fifty states, Washington, D.C., and Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_poverty_rate). This is attributed to several factors, including the state's proximity to the major economic centers of New York City and [Philadelphia](50585.md), its hosting the highest number of [millionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millionaire) both per capita and per square mile in the U.S., and the fact that it has the most [scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientist) and [engineers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engineer) per square mile in the world.[^cite_note-Nsci-87][^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89]

According to the [U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development)'s 2022 [Annual Homeless Assessment Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_Homeless_Assessment_Report_to_Congress), there were an estimated 8,752 [homeless](19394651.md) people in New Jersey.[^cite_note-90] The top countries of origin for New Jersey's immigrants in 2018 were India, [Dominican Republic](8060.md), [Mexico](3966054.md), [Ecuador](9334.md), and the [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipinos_in_New_Jersey).[^cite_note-91]

### Race and ethnicity



Ethnic origins in New Jersey



Largest alone or in any combination ethnic origin by county in New Jersey, per the 2020 census



Largest ethnicity by municipality based on 2020 census

New Jersey is one of the most ethnically diverse states in the United States: as of 2022, over one-fifth (21.5%) of its residents are Hispanic or Latino, 15.3% are Black, and one-tenth are Asian. One in four New Jerseyans were born abroad and more than one million (12.1%) are not fully fluent in English. As of the 2024 census estimate, the White non-Hispanic population of the state was less than 50%, making it a [majority-minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majority_minority_in_the_United_States) state.[^cite_note-CensusACS2024-92] Compared to the U.S. as a whole, the state is more racially and ethnically diverse and has a higher proportion of immigrants.[^cite_note-93]

New Jersey is home to roughly half a million [undocumented immigrants](5044573.md),[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112] comprising an estimated 6.2% of the population, which in 2018 was the fifth-highest percentage of any U.S. state.[^cite_note-113] The municipalities of Camden, Jersey City, and Newark are considered [sanctuary cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctuary_city) for illegal immigrants.[^cite_note-114]

New Jersey is one of the most ethnically and religiously [diverse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyethnicity) states in the United States. As of 2024, a quarter of New Jerseyans (25%) were [foreign born](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_born), the second most after [California](5407.md), compared to the national average of 14.8%.[^cite_note-Foreign-23] As of 2011, 56.4% of New Jersey's children under the age of one belonged to racial or ethnic minority groups, meaning that they had at least one parent who was not non-Hispanic white.[^cite_note-115] The 2019 Vintage Year Census estimated that the state's ethnic makeup was as follows: 71.9% White alone, 15.1% Black or African American alone, 10.0% Asian alone, 0.6% American Indian and Alaska Native alone, 0.1% Native Hawaiian and Other Pacific Islander alone, and 2.3% Two or more races. Hispanic or Latino accounted for 20.9%, while White alone (non-Hispanic or Latino) accounted for 54.6% of the population.[^cite_note-quickfacts-79] Many of the municipalities in [Bergen County, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bergen_County,_New_Jersey), the state's largest county, have a sizeable minority population of Hispanics and Asians.[^cite_note-116] There are also three [state-recognized tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-recognized_tribes_in_the_United_States), and in 2020, 51,186 identified as being Native American alone, while 96,691 did in combination with one or more other races.[^cite_note-Race_and_Ethnicity_2010-2020-94]

New Jersey hosts some of the nation's largest communities of religious and ethnic minorities in proportional or absolute terms. It has the second-largest [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_American) population by percentage (after New York);[^cite_note-117] the largest [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) and [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_the_United_States) populations by percentage;[^cite_note-Muslims_by_state-118] the largest population of [Peruvians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peruvian_American) in the U.S.; the largest population of [Cubans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Americans) outside [Florida](18933066.md); the third-highest [Asian](689.md) population by percentage; and the second-highest [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Americans_in_New_York_City) population,[^cite_note-Italians-119] according to the [2000 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_census). African Americans, [Hispanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans) ([Puerto Ricans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Rican_American) and [Dominicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_American)), [West Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indian_Americans), [Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_American), and [Brazilian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_people) and [Portuguese Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Americans) are also high in number. New Jersey also has the fourth-largest [Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_American) population, and fourth-largest [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_American) population, per the 2010 U.S. Census.

New Jersey has the-third highest [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_American#List_of_U.S._States_by_population_of_Asian_Indians) population of any state by absolute numbers and the [highest by percentage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indians_in_the_New_York_metropolitan_area),[^cite_note-120][^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122][^cite_note-censusbrief-123] with [India Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_Square) in [Jersey City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_City,_New_Jersey), [Hudson County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_County,_New_Jersey)[^cite_note-Kiniry,_Laura_2006._pg._3422-110] hosting the highest concentration of Asian Indians in the [Western Hemisphere](73162.md).[^cite_note-Laryssa_Wirstiuk22-124] A study by the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) found that in 2013, New Jersey was the only U.S. state in which immigrants born in [India](14533.md) constituted the largest [foreign-born](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_born) nationality, representing roughly 10% of all foreign-born residents in the state.[^cite_note-IndiaNewJersey-125] [Central New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_New_Jersey), particularly [Edison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edison_Township,_New_Jersey) and surrounding [Middlesex County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middlesex_County,_New_Jersey), has the highest concentration of Indians, at nearly 20% in 2020; [Little India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_India_\(Edison/Iselin\)) is the largest and most diverse [South Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian) cultural hub in the United States.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129] The area includes a sprawling [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_in_New_York_City) and [Koreatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Americans_in_New_York_City) running along [New Jersey Route 27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_Route_27).[^cite_note-130] [Monroe Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe_Township,_Middlesex_County,_New_Jersey) in Middlesex County has experienced a particularly rapid growth rate in its [Indian American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indians_in_the_New_York_City_metropolitan_region) population with an estimated 5,943 (13.6%) as of 2017,[^cite_note-IndiansMonroe-131] which was 23 times the 256 (0.9%) counted at the 2000 Census; [Diwali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diwali) is celebrated by the township as a [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) holiday. In Middlesex County, election [ballots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballot) are printed in English, [Spanish](26825.md), [Gujarati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujarati_language), [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi), and [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language).[^cite_note-132] [Robbinsville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robbinsville,_New_Jersey), in neighboring Mercer County, hosts [the world's largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swaminarayan_Akshardham_\(North_America\)) [Hindu temple](2247213.md) outside Asia.[^cite_note-World'sLargestHinduTempleNJ-133] [Carteret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carteret,_New_Jersey)'s [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_people) [Sikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh) community, variously estimated at upwards of 3,000, is the largest concentration of Sikhs in the state.[^cite_note-134] [Bergen County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bergen_County,_New_Jersey) is home to America's largest [Malayali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayali) community.[^cite_note-135]

New Jersey also has the third-largest [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_American) population, with Bergen County home to the highest Korean concentration per capita of any U.S. county[^cite_note-Richard_Newman-136] (6.9% in 2011). It is a growing hub and home to [all of the nation's top ten municipalities by percentage of Korean population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._cities_with_significant_Korean-American_populations#Top_ten_municipalities_as_ranked_by_Korean-American_percentage_of_overall_population_in_2010),[^cite_note-BergenCountyKoreanPercentage-137] led by [Palisades Park (벼랑 공원)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreatown,_Palisades_Park),[^cite_note-books.google.com-138] the municipality with the highest [density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._cities_with_significant_Korean-American_populations#Municipalities_with_density_of_at_least_500_Korean_Americans_per_square_mile_in_2010) of ethnic [Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koreans) in the [Western Hemisphere](73162.md). Displaying ubiquitous [Hangul (한글)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hangul) signage and known as the *Korean village*,[^cite_note-Palisades_Park_Municipal_Court-139] Palisades Park uniquely comprises a Korean majority (52% in 2010) of its population,[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141] with both the [highest Korean-American density and percentage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._cities_with_significant_Korean-American_populations#Municipalities_with_density_of_at_least_500_Korean_Americans_per_square_mile_in_2010) of any municipality in the United States.

As of 2010, 71.31% (5,830,812) of New Jersey residents age 5 and older spoke English at home as a [primary language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_language), while 14.59% (1,193,261) spoke Spanish, 1.23% (100,217) Chinese (which includes [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese) and [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Chinese)), 1.06% (86,849) Italian, 1.06% (86,486) [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language), 0.96% (78,627) [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language), and [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_language) was spoken as a [main language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_language) by 0.89% (73,057) of the population over the age of five. In total, 28.69% (2,345,644) of New Jersey's population age 5 and older spoke a [mother language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_language) other than English.[^cite_note-MLA_Data-153]

A diverse collection of languages has since evolved amongst the state's population, given that New Jersey has become [cosmopolitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism) and is home to [ethnic enclaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_enclave) of non-English-speaking communities:[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157]

- [Albanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_language) – [Paterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paterson,_New_Jersey), [Garfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garfield,_New_Jersey)

- [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language) – [Bayonne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayonne,_New_Jersey), Paterson, [Jersey City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_City,_New_Jersey)

- [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language) – [Bergen County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bergen_County,_New_Jersey)

- [Bengali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language) – Paterson

- [Gujarati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujarati_language) and [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi) – Jersey City, all of Middlesex County, Cherry Hill, [Parsippany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsippany,_New_Jersey), [Princeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton,_New_Jersey)

- [Indonesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_language) – [Gloucester City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloucester_City,_New_Jersey), [Middlesex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middlesex_County,_New_Jersey), [Somerset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somerset_County,_New_Jersey), and [Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_County,_New_Jersey) counties

- Japanese – [Edgewater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgewater,_New_Jersey) and [Fort Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Lee,_New_Jersey) [boroughs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borough_\(New_Jersey\)) in Bergen County

- [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_language) – [Bergen County (numerous municipalities)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bergen_County,_New_Jersey#Korean_American); [Edison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edison,_New_Jersey); and [Cherry Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherry_Hill,_New_Jersey#Demographics)

- [Macedonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonian_language) – Bergen County

- [Malayalam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayalam) – Bergen County

- [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language) – Bergen County ([Garfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garfield,_New_Jersey), [Wallington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallington,_New_Jersey)); Mercer County ([Top Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_Road,_Trenton,_New_Jersey), [Lawrence Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Township,_Mercer_County,_New_Jersey), [Hopewell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hopewell,_New_Jersey)); [Linden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linden,_New_Jersey)

- [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_language) and [Telugu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telugu_language) – [Edison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edison,_New_Jersey), [Monroe Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe_Township,_Middlesex_County,_New_Jersey) (*Edison-South*), and all of Middlesex County; Fair Lawn, Parsippany

### Sexual orientation and gender identity

Further information: [LGBT culture in New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_culture_in_New_York_City) and [LGBT culture in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_culture_in_Philadelphia)

New Jersey is widely regarded as an [LGBTQ+ friendly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay-friendly) state and is now home to more [gay villages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_gay_villages#United_States) per square mile than any other U.S. state. [Same-sex marriage in New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_New_Jersey) has been legally recognized since October 21, 2013, the effective date of a trial court ruling invalidating New Jersey's restriction of [marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage) to persons of different sexes at the time. In September 2013, Mary C. Jacobson, Assignment Judge of the [Mercer Vicinage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercer_County,_New_Jersey) of the [Superior Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_Superior_Court), ruled that as a result of the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md)'s June 2013 decision in *[United States v. Windsor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Windsor)*, the [Constitution of New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_New_Jersey) requires the state to recognize [same-sex marriages](92656.md).[^cite_note-166]

Numerous [gayborhoods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gayborhood) have emerged in New Jersey, most prominently in [Jersey City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_City),[^cite_note-NJGayVillages-167] [Asbury Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asbury_Park,_New_Jersey), [Maplewood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maplewood,_New_Jersey),[^cite_note-168] [Montclair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montclair,_New_Jersey), and [Lambertville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambertville,_New_Jersey).[^cite_note-169] [Trenton](57843.md), the [state capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._state_capitals) of New Jersey, elected [Reed Gusciora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reed_Gusciora), its first openly gay mayor, in 2018,[^cite_note-170] and Jennifer Williams, New Jersey's first openly [transgender](19904525.md) city councilmember, in 2022.[^cite_note-171] In June 2018, Maplewood, [Essex County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essex_County,_New_Jersey) unveiled permanent [rainbow-colored crosswalks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainbow_crossing) to celebrate LGBTQ pride.[^cite_note-172] [Rahway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahway,_New_Jersey), [Union County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_County,_New_Jersey), also unveiled its own rainbow-colored crosswalks in June 2019.[^cite_note-173] In January 2019, [New Jersey Governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_New_Jersey) [Phil Murphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phil_Murphy) signed legislation mandating LGBTQ+ inclusive [educational curriculum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curriculum) in schools.[^cite_note-174] In February 2019, New Jersey began allowing a [neutral or non-binary gender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_neutrality) choice on [birth certificates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_certificate).[^cite_note-175]

See also: [Category\:Religion in New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Religion_in_New_Jersey)

By number of adherents, the largest religious traditions in New Jersey, according to the 2010 [Association of Religion Data Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Religion_Data_Archives), were the [Roman Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church) with 3,235,290; [Islam](6037917.md) with 160,666; and the [United Methodist Church](32304.md) with 138,052.\[^cite_note-[www.thearda.com-177](http://www.thearda.com-177/)] The world's [largest Hindu temple outside Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_Hindu_temples) is in [Robbinsville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robbinsville,_New_Jersey), [Mercer County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercer_County,_New_Jersey).[^cite_note-World'sLargestHinduTempleNJ-133] In September 2021, the State of New Jersey aligned with the World Hindu Council to declare October [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) Heritage Month. In January 2018, [Gurbir Grewal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurbir_Grewal) became the first [Sikh American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_American) to serve as [state attorney general](6065438.md) in the United States.[^cite_note-GurbirGrewalNJ61stAttorneyGeneral-178] In January 2019, [Sadaf Jaffer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadaf_Jaffer) of [Montgomery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery,_New_Jersey) became the first female [Muslim American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_American) mayor, first female [South Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian) mayor, and first female [Pakistani-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani-American) mayor in the U.S.[^cite_note-179] Large numbers of Orthodox Jews are now migrating to New Jersey from New York, due to the latter's higher cost of living.[^cite_note-NYLargestOrthodoxJewishCommunities-180] The world's largest gathering of rabbis outside of Israel occurred in [Edison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edison,_New_Jersey) on December 1, 2024.[^cite_note-LargestJewishgatheringWorldCentralNewJersey-181]

- Cathedral Basilica of the Sacred Heart in Newark, the fifth-largest cathedral in North America, is the seat of the city's Roman Catholic archdiocese.

  [Cathedral Basilica of the Sacred Heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_Basilica_of_the_Sacred_Heart_\(Newark\)) in [Newark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newark,_New_Jersey), the fifth-largest cathedral in North America, is the seat of the city's [Roman Catholic archdiocese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Newark).

- Beth Medrash Govoha (Hebrew: בית מדרש גבוה), in Lakewood, Ocean County, the world's largest Jewish yeshiva outside Israel. New Jersey is home to the second-highest Jewish American population per capita, after New York, and the fastest-growing Orthodox Jewish population.

  [Beth Medrash Govoha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beth_Medrash_Govoha) ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): בית מדרש גבוה), in [Lakewood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakewood_Township,_New_Jersey), [Ocean County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocean_County,_New_Jersey), the world's largest [Jewish](15624.md) [yeshiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeshiva) outside Israel. New Jersey is home to the second-highest [Jewish American](1131183.md#Demographics) population per capita, after [New York](8210131.md), and the fastest-growing [Orthodox Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism) population.

  [^cite_note-LargestYeshivaOutsideIsrael-182]

  [^cite_note-LargestYeshivaNorthAmerica-183]

- Akshardam New Jersey in Robbinsville, Mercer County, the world's second-largest Hindu temple and the largest in the Western Hemisphere. New Jersey is home to the highest Hindu population per capita in the U.S.

  [Akshardam New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akshardam_New_Jersey) in [Robbinsville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robbinsville,_New_Jersey), [Mercer County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercer_County,_New_Jersey), the world's second-largest [Hindu temple](2247213.md) and the [largest in the](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_Hindu_temples) [Western Hemisphere](73162.md). New Jersey is home to the highest [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) population per capita in the U.S.

- Islamic Center of Passaic County, Paterson, Passaic County, was founded in 1990. New Jersey is home to one of the highest Muslim population concentrations in the Western Hemisphere (3.5%), and Paterson, which houses the Islamic Center of Passaic County, is the epicenter of New Jersey's Muslim community, leading South Paterson to be nicknamed Little Istanbul and Little Ramallah.

  [Islamic Center of Passaic County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Center_of_Passaic_County), [Paterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paterson,_New_Jersey), [Passaic County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passaic_County,_New_Jersey), was founded in 1990. New Jersey is home to one of the highest Muslim population concentrations in the [Western Hemisphere](73162.md) (3.5%), and Paterson, which houses the Islamic Center of Passaic County, is the epicenter of New Jersey's Muslim community, leading [South Paterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Paterson) to be nicknamed *Little [Istanbul](3391396.md)* and *Little [Ramallah](44132.md)*.

  [^cite_note-Muslims_by_state-118]
